English for Work · Industry & infrastructure

Plumbing Workplace English

Identify fixtures and pipe specifications, reconcile layouts, describe moisture evidence, explain drainage access, coordinate visits, and clarify estimates and handover records.

For Plumbers, supervised plumbing apprentices, plumbing service coordinators, and plumbing crew leads.

Copy complete AI prompts ↓

What you will practice · Choose your pace

What you will practice

  • Clarify a customer report using fixture, location, and observation time without turning a symptom into a diagnosis.
  • Distinguish nominal size from measured outside diameter and request complete product identification before judging a delivery.
  • Explain a dimension conflict by naming both reference surfaces and requesting a coordinated detail without inventing an offset.
  • Report the observed condition with a time limit, acknowledge a customer concern, and avoid unsupported cause or repair claims.
  • Explain a technical term in ordinary language and distinguish a design request from an approved change to service access.

Choose your pace

Quick practice · 15 minutes: complete one short exchange, check the explanations, and read both roles aloud.

Extended practice · two or three sittings: check the case facts, study the vocabulary and phrases, complete the longer conversation, then rehearse the follow-up exchange.

Level guide: designed for intermediate to advanced learners. B1 learners can start with the short exchanges and supplied phrases; B2 learners can complete the cloze conversations; C1 learners can focus on the distinctions in the language checks. These are teaching suggestions, not a certified level assessment.

Your eight lessons

01 Identifying fixtures and service history02 Clarifying pipe and component specifications03 Reconciling fixture layouts and dimensions04 Describing moisture without diagnosing its source05 Explaining drainage and access terminology06 Coordinating interruptions and room access07 Pricing changes and restoration boundaries08 Handing over plumbing records and follow-up
Prompts

The cases, products, and project records are fictional. This book teaches English, not plumbing diagnosis, installation, testing, repair, valve operation, or code compliance. Follow actual training, authorization, manufacturer instructions, site procedures, and applicable requirements. A reported symptom, dry surface, drawing, or handover document does not establish system condition or authorize work. The conversations do not instruct clients to test, dismantle, or operate plumbing equipment.

01Identifying fixtures and service historyWhich sink in Unit 3B?+

Your goal

Clarify a customer report using fixture, location, and observation time without turning a symptom into a diagnosis.

This lesson in the book, page 4 ↗

A Read the situation

Which sink in Unit 3B?

Tenant Mina reports a sink problem in Unit 3B. A bathroom basin and a utility sink stand beside one another. She means the bathroom basin and noticed water on the cabinet base at 07:00. The source of the water has not been established. Service coordinator Ellis needs an accurate description for the plumber, using what Mina has already observed. No earlier repair history is supplied, and no instruction to operate or dismantle equipment is needed.

Mina: Tenant / Ellis: Service coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. Which fixture does Mina mean?

Answer and explanation

A. The bathroom basin in Unit 3B

The brief identifies the bathroom basin, not the nearby utility sink.

2. What does 07:00 identify?

Answer and explanation

B. When Mina noticed water

The supplied time is an observation time, not a verified onset.

3. What is known about the source?

Answer and explanation

D. It has not been established

Water location alone does not identify its source in this report.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

Water was noticed on the cabinet base at 07:00. This does not establish when it first appeared or which component caused it. Keep the fixture identity, observed location, and time together.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
fixture
Plumbing appliance such as a basin, sink, or toilet.identify the fixture
bathroom basin
Bowl used for washing hands or face in a bathroom.specify the bathroom basin
utility sink
Sink intended for cleaning or general utility tasks.distinguish the utility sink
vanity unit
Bathroom cabinet assembly associated with a basin.describe the vanity unit
cabinet base
Bottom surface within the cabinet.report water on the cabinet base
service call
Request or attendance for a reported plumbing issue.log a service call
tenant report
Account given by the person occupying the premises.record the tenant report
unit number
Identifier of an individual apartment or premises.confirm the unit number
fixture location
Position of the particular plumbing appliance.state the fixture location
observation time
Time when someone noticed a condition.record the observation time
onset
Point when a condition actually began.distinguish onset from observation
source
Origin of the reported water or problem.establish the source
supply connection
Connection associated with water delivery to a fixture.identify a supply connection
waste connection
Connection associated with carrying used water away.describe the waste connection
trap
Fitting designed to retain a liquid seal in a drainage system.identify the basin trap
faucet
Fitting that controls delivery of water at a fixture.name the faucet
tap
Common alternative term for a faucet.clarify the tap description
visible moisture
Wetness that can be seen at the reported location.describe visible moisture
service history
Record of earlier attendance or work.check the service history
previous repair
Corrective work completed on an earlier occasion.confirm a previous repair
reported symptom
Condition described by a customer before assessment.retain the reported symptom
call summary
Concise record of information from the conversation.read back the call summary
static pressure
In this plumbing report, pressure at a point with no flow.distinguish static from flowing pressure
attendance
Visit by the relevant service person.arrange attendance

The bathroom basin and utility sink are different fixtures. Familiar terms such as tap and faucet can be clarified without correcting the customer unnecessarily. No previous repair or component failure is established here.

C Notice the language

Clarify without making the customer defend a diagnosis

Customers may use one everyday name for several fixtures. Offer concrete alternatives, acknowledge the reported condition, and ask about existing observations. A precise service description helps the next person without requiring the customer to identify a failed component.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Offer two concrete choices
Do you mean the bathroom basin or the utility sink?
Confirm the premises
I have the location as Unit 3B.
Separate adjacent fixtures
The utility sink is beside it, but it is not the fixture you mean.
Locate the observation
Where had you already noticed the water?
Preserve the time
You first noticed it at 07:00.
Avoid changing the onset
I will record that as the observation time.
Name the surface
The water was on the cabinet base.
Keep the report attributed
The tenant reports water beneath the bathroom basin.
Keep the cause open
The source has not been established.
Avoid naming a failed part
I cannot identify a failed connection from that description.
Distinguish the routes
A supply connection and a waste connection are different.
Clarify familiar wording
By tap, do you mean the basin faucet?
Record the history limit
No earlier repair history has been supplied.
Avoid inventing reassurance
I cannot say the issue has been resolved.
Read back the details
Bathroom basin, Unit 3B, cabinet base, noticed at 07:00.
Close with the record
I will pass that description to the plumber.
6 language and precision notes
Do you mean X or Y?
Offers bounded alternatives that resolve an ambiguous noun.
Noticed at
Records an observation without asserting when the problem began.
Reports
Attributes an account to its source.
Beneath versus from
Beneath gives location; from asserts origin.
Has not been established
Leaves a cause open without denying the observation.
No history supplied
Does not mean that no earlier work ever occurred.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which question best clarifies the fixture?

Answer and explanation

D. Do you mean the bathroom basin or the utility sink beside it?

The alternatives identify the fixture without assuming a failure or requesting work.

2. Which summary preserves the time accurately?

Answer and explanation

D. Water noticed on the cabinet base at 07:00

Noticed records the tenant's observation rather than onset or scheduling.

3. Which statement wrongly converts location into cause?

Answer and explanation

D. The water must be coming from the trap

The trap is not identified as the source by the supplied facts.

4. What should accompany the handoff?

Answer and explanation

B. Fixture, unit, observed surface, time, and unresolved source

These details preserve the usable report without adding unsupported history or conclusions.

Check explanations in the book, page 85 ↗

E Conversations

Which sink in Unit 3B?

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Mina: Tenant / Ellis: Service coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • observation time
  • attendance
  • source
  • faucet
  • bathroom basin
  • call summary
  • service history
  • reported symptom
  • fixture
  • cabinet base
  1. Mina
    Hi, it's the sink in Unit 3B. There was water underneath it when I went into the bathroom this morning. Can you log a call?
  2. Ellis
    Of course. Which 1 do you mean: the bathroom basin where you wash your hands, or the utility sink beside it?
  3. Mina
    The bathroom one. The utility sink is next to the cabinet, so I can see why just saying sink isn't very helpful.
  4. Ellis
    I'll name the 2. When you say underneath, where was the water you had already seen: inside the cabinet or somewhere else?
  5. Mina
    Inside, on the bottom of the cabinet. I noticed it at seven. I don't know how long it had been there before that.
  6. Ellis
    Water on the 3, noticed at 07:00. That's a useful location and time. Have you identified where it came from?
  7. Mina
    No. I almost said the pipe was leaking, but I haven't seen which part the water came from. Can you leave that open?
  8. Ellis
    Yes. I'll record the 4 as unestablished. We won't turn water beneath a basin into a diagnosis of a failed pipe or connection.
  9. Mina
    The form has a box for when it started. Seven is when I noticed it, but I can't honestly say that was the start.
  10. Ellis
    I'll keep it as the 5 and leave onset unconfirmed. That avoids giving the plumber a more precise history than you can support.
  11. Mina
    There's another box for earlier repairs. I don't have any records. Does leaving that blank mean the basin has never been repaired?
  12. Ellis
    No. I'll mark 6 as not supplied. Missing information isn't evidence that there was never earlier work, so we shouldn't select no previous repairs.
  13. Mina
    One naming question: when I say tap, I mean the fitting above the basin. Is that what your form calls the faucet?
  14. Ellis
    Yes, 7 is another common name for that water-delivery fitting. Mentioning it doesn't make it the source of the water you've reported.
  15. Mina
    Good. I don't want someone arriving with a note that says the tap is broken. I haven't established that at all.
  16. Ellis
    I'll retain your 8 instead: water beneath the identified basin. The plumber can assess the cause; you don't need to dismantle anything for this call.
  17. Mina
    Could you read the request back? Especially the location. I don't want the two sinks mixed up when the plumber sees the job.
  18. Ellis
    The 9 is Unit 3B, bathroom basin, water on the cabinet base, noticed at 07:00. Source unestablished; earlier repair history not supplied.
  19. Mina
    That's right. It includes what I saw and doesn't add a cause. I haven't been given a visit time yet, either.
  20. Ellis
    I'll pass it through to arrange 10. I haven't confirmed a time in this conversation, so please don't treat seven as the plumber's arrival time.

Answers and explanations
  1. fixture Turn 2. Fixture names the particular appliance; the room contains two different sinks.
  2. bathroom basin Turn 4. Bathroom basin resolves the two-fixture ambiguity using Mina's explicit clarification.
  3. cabinet base Turn 6. Cabinet base identifies the observed surface without asserting where the water originated.
  4. source Turn 8. Source concerns origin, which Mina's observation has not established.
  5. observation time Turn 10. Observation time preserves when Mina noticed water without inventing the onset.
  6. service history Turn 12. Service history concerns earlier work; none has been supplied, not proven absent.
  7. faucet Turn 14. Faucet is the alternative name for the water-delivery fitting, not a diagnosis.
  8. reported symptom Turn 16. Reported symptom distinguishes Mina's account from a confirmed component failure.
  9. call summary Turn 18. Call summary gathers fixture, location, observation, and uncertainty for the next person.
  10. attendance Turn 20. Attendance means the service visit being arranged, without promising a diagnosis or time.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Mina: Hi, it's the sink in Unit 3B. There was water underneath it when I went into the bathroom this morning. Can you log a call?
  2. Ellis: Of course. Which fixture do you mean: the bathroom basin where you wash your hands, or the utility sink beside it?
  3. Mina: The bathroom one. The utility sink is next to the cabinet, so I can see why just saying sink isn't very helpful.
  4. Ellis: I'll name the bathroom basin. When you say underneath, where was the water you had already seen: inside the cabinet or somewhere else?
  5. Mina: Inside, on the bottom of the cabinet. I noticed it at seven. I don't know how long it had been there before that.
  6. Ellis: Water on the cabinet base, noticed at 07:00. That's a useful location and time. Have you identified where it came from?
  7. Mina: No. I almost said the pipe was leaking, but I haven't seen which part the water came from. Can you leave that open?
  8. Ellis: Yes. I'll record the source as unestablished. We won't turn water beneath a basin into a diagnosis of a failed pipe or connection.
  9. Mina: The form has a box for when it started. Seven is when I noticed it, but I can't honestly say that was the start.
  10. Ellis: I'll keep it as the observation time and leave onset unconfirmed. That avoids giving the plumber a more precise history than you can support.
  11. Mina: There's another box for earlier repairs. I don't have any records. Does leaving that blank mean the basin has never been repaired?
  12. Ellis: No. I'll mark service history as not supplied. Missing information isn't evidence that there was never earlier work, so we shouldn't select no previous repairs.
  13. Mina: One naming question: when I say tap, I mean the fitting above the basin. Is that what your form calls the faucet?
  14. Ellis: Yes, faucet is another common name for that water-delivery fitting. Mentioning it doesn't make it the source of the water you've reported.
  15. Mina: Good. I don't want someone arriving with a note that says the tap is broken. I haven't established that at all.
  16. Ellis: I'll retain your reported symptom instead: water beneath the identified basin. The plumber can assess the cause; you don't need to dismantle anything for this call.
  17. Mina: Could you read the request back? Especially the location. I don't want the two sinks mixed up when the plumber sees the job.
  18. Ellis: The call summary is Unit 3B, bathroom basin, water on the cabinet base, noticed at 07:00. Source unestablished; earlier repair history not supplied.
  19. Mina: That's right. It includes what I saw and doesn't add a cause. I haven't been given a visit time yet, either.
  20. Ellis: I'll pass it through to arrange attendance. I haven't confirmed a time in this conversation, so please don't treat seven as the plumber's arrival time.
Dialogue in the book, page 10 ↗
Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

Which sink in Unit 3B?

A plumber clarifies the fictional Unit 3B report with its tenant.

  1. Plumber: Your request says bathroom sink. Do you mean the basin or the utility sink nearby?
  2. Tenant: The basin inside the bathroom. There was water on its cabinet base at seven.
  3. Plumber: Thank you. That locates the observation, but it doesn't establish where the water came from.
  4. Tenant: The manager mentioned a riser leak. Is that the same thing as my basin?
  5. Plumber: A riser is vertical pipework; a branch line serves part of the system. Neither source is confirmed.
  6. Tenant: I can give you the earlier service number for the basin if that helps.
  7. Plumber: Please do. It will help us connect the history to the correct fixture.
  8. Tenant: Should I describe this as the same fault returning from the previous visit?
  9. Plumber: Say what you noticed today. Whether it shares the earlier cause still needs assessment.
  10. Tenant: I'll give the current observation and the old reference separately so neither is lost.
Two washrooms with identical basins

A plumber and office manager identify a service location.

  1. Plumber: The request names the rear washroom, but both corridors have a washroom at the rear.
  2. Office manager: I mean the one beyond the staff kitchen, not the one beside the loading entrance.
  3. Plumber: Could we use the room identifier from your floor plan to remove that ambiguity?
  4. Office manager: Yes, that's W14. The basin nearest the door is the one our cleaner reported.
  5. Plumber: I'll include W14 and nearest the door, with the cleaner identified as the reporter.
  6. Office manager: The other basin was serviced last month. Should I include that record as well?
  7. Plumber: You can provide it as background, but we shouldn't merge the two fixture histories.
  8. Office manager: Understood. I'll label last month's record with the other basin's location before sending it.
  9. Plumber: Who can arrange access to W14 during the service visit so the plumber isn't delayed?
  10. Office manager: Our caretaker will meet you at reception and confirm the room with you in person.
A complaint changes meaning in retelling

A plumber checks a hotel supervisor's summary of a guest report.

  1. Plumber: The booking says the shower is blocked. What did the guest actually report to reception?
  2. Hotel supervisor: They said water remained on the shower floor longer than usual after yesterday's use.
  3. Plumber: I'll retain that description. Blocked sounds like a diagnosis rather than the guest's observation.
  4. Hotel supervisor: That's fair. The receptionist shortened the message because the service form had limited space.
  5. Plumber: Do you have the room number and the time the guest noticed the issue?
  6. Hotel supervisor: Room 418, yesterday evening. I can ask reception for a more precise time if available.
  7. Plumber: Please also keep any earlier service reference separate from the current report of slow drainage.
  8. Hotel supervisor: Should we ask the guest to reproduce it before the plumber attends the room?
  9. Plumber: No additional operation is needed for this conversation. Their existing account gives us the enquiry.
  10. Hotel supervisor: I'll correct the description and arrange access through housekeeping, with the source still unconfirmed.

F Say it

Two scenarios to practice

Which sink in Unit 3B?
  1. Read turns 1-10 in pairs. Stress bathroom, cabinet base, and noticed at seven to keep fixture, location, and time distinct.
  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep not supplied separate from never repaired, and arrange attendance separate from a booked time.

On your own: read both roles. With a partner: swap roles after the first reading. Use the completed conversation to check any line you need to repeat.

Give the plumber the precise report

Complete the handoff using the tenant account. Keep the fixture, observed surface, time, and cause status distinct.

Choose one reply for each gap, then read the four turns in order. Check the explanation after each choice.

1. Coordinator: "The tenant means the bathroom ___ in Unit 3B."

Answer and explanation

A. basin

Basin identifies the fixture Mina selected, not the adjacent utility sink.

2. Plumber: "Water was seen on the cabinet ___."

Answer and explanation

C. base

Base names the observed surface without identifying the water source.

3. Coordinator: "It was noticed at ___."

Answer and explanation

A. 07:00

The time belongs to the observation, not a proven onset.

4. Plumber: "The source remains ___."

Answer and explanation

A. unestablished

The account supplies no assessment establishing which component caused the water.

Read the completed exchange twice, switching roles. Keep the printed facts and professional responsibilities unchanged.

Build vocabulary and collocations — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Retrieve occupational vocabulary
1. Select four terms from the reference. Explain each in plain English, give two natural collocations and quote or create a short workplace example. Label invented examples. Contrast each term with a plausible confusable term; acknowledge regional variation.
2. Present one role-labelled exchange with one missing word or phrase and four choices A-D. Exactly one choice must fit the stated facts and grammar. Stop and wait for my answer before revealing it.
3. Explain all four options, then invite me to read the completed exchange aloud. Offer a sentence frame for optional spoken practice, never an open-ended writing assignment.
4. Repeat for the other three terms, one question at a time. Vary the correct letter and the situation; preserve the reference facts. If a choice is ambiguous, replace the item.
5. Finish with two new four-choice collocation questions for later review, with a clearly separate answer key. Do not claim to schedule a reminder.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

Practice setting: B2 independent. Use natural professional English with brief explanations. Let me attempt each task without a model first. Offer a hint after difficulty and invite a more precise retry.

Lesson 01: Identifying fixtures and service history
Original fictional case: Tenant Mina reports a sink problem in Unit 3B. A bathroom basin and a utility sink stand beside one another. She means the bathroom basin and noticed water on the cabinet base at 07:00. The source of the water has not been established. Service coordinator Ellis needs an accurate description for the plumber, using what Mina has already observed. No earlier repair history is supplied, and no instruction to operate or dismantle equipment is needed.
Language workshop: Which sink in Unit 3B?
Communication goal: Clarify a customer report using fixture, location, and observation time without turning a symptom into a diagnosis.
Language guidance: Water was noticed on the cabinet base at 07:00. This does not establish when it first appeared or which component caused it. Keep the fixture identity, observed location, and time together.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Do you mean the bathroom basin or the utility sink? / I have the location as Unit 3B. / The utility sink is beside it, but it is not the fixture you mean. / Where had you already noticed the water? / You first noticed it at 07:00. / I will record that as the observation time. / The water was on the cabinet base. / The tenant reports water beneath the bathroom basin. / The source has not been established. / I cannot identify a failed connection from that description. / A supply connection and a waste connection are different. / By tap, do you mean the basin faucet? / No earlier repair history has been supplied. / I cannot say the issue has been resolved. / Bathroom basin, Unit 3B, cabinet base, noticed at 07:00. / I will pass that description to the plumber.
Vocabulary: fixture: Plumbing appliance such as a basin, sink, or toilet. / bathroom basin: Bowl used for washing hands or face in a bathroom. / utility sink: Sink intended for cleaning or general utility tasks. / vanity unit: Bathroom cabinet assembly associated with a basin. / cabinet base: Bottom surface within the cabinet. / service call: Request or attendance for a reported plumbing issue. / tenant report: Account given by the person occupying the premises. / unit number: Identifier of an individual apartment or premises. / fixture location: Position of the particular plumbing appliance. / observation time: Time when someone noticed a condition. / onset: Point when a condition actually began. / source: Origin of the reported water or problem. / supply connection: Connection associated with water delivery to a fixture. / waste connection: Connection associated with carrying used water away. / trap: Fitting designed to retain a liquid seal in a drainage system. / faucet: Fitting that controls delivery of water at a fixture. / tap: Common alternative term for a faucet. / visible moisture: Wetness that can be seen at the reported location. / service history: Record of earlier attendance or work. / previous repair: Corrective work completed on an earlier occasion. / reported symptom: Condition described by a customer before assessment. / call summary: Concise record of information from the conversation. / static pressure: In this plumbing report, pressure at a point with no flow. / attendance: Visit by the relevant service person.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 in pairs. Stress bathroom, cabinet base, and noticed at seven to keep fixture, location, and time distinct. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep not supplied separate from never repaired, and arrange attendance separate from a booked time. Complete and check the handoff exchange. Read it twice, preserving basin, base, 07:00, and unestablished.
Partner: Mina: Tenant / Ellis: Service coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the handoff using the tenant account. Keep the fixture, observed surface, time, and cause status distinct.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mina: Could you read the request back? Especially the location. I don't want the two sinks mixed up when the plumber sees the job.
Ellis: The call summary is Unit 3B, bathroom basin, water on the cabinet base, noticed at 07:00. Source unestablished; earlier repair history not supplied.

END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Lesson 01: Identifying fixtures and service history
Original fictional case: Tenant Mina reports a sink problem in Unit 3B. A bathroom basin and a utility sink stand beside one another. She means the bathroom basin and noticed water on the cabinet base at 07:00. The source of the water has not been established. Service coordinator Ellis needs an accurate description for the plumber, using what Mina has already observed. No earlier repair history is supplied, and no instruction to operate or dismantle equipment is needed.
Language workshop: Which sink in Unit 3B?
Communication goal: Clarify a customer report using fixture, location, and observation time without turning a symptom into a diagnosis.
Language guidance: Water was noticed on the cabinet base at 07:00. This does not establish when it first appeared or which component caused it. Keep the fixture identity, observed location, and time together.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): Do you mean the bathroom basin or the utility sink? / I have the location as Unit 3B. / The utility sink is beside it, but it is not the fixture you mean. / Where had you already noticed the water? / You first noticed it at 07:00. / I will record that as the observation time. / The water was on the cabinet base. / The tenant reports water beneath the bathroom basin. / The source has not been established. / I cannot identify a failed connection from that description. / A supply connection and a waste connection are different. / By tap, do you mean the basin faucet? / No earlier repair history has been supplied. / I cannot say the issue has been resolved. / Bathroom basin, Unit 3B, cabinet base, noticed at 07:00. / I will pass that description to the plumber.
Vocabulary: fixture: Plumbing appliance such as a basin, sink, or toilet. / bathroom basin: Bowl used for washing hands or face in a bathroom. / utility sink: Sink intended for cleaning or general utility tasks. / vanity unit: Bathroom cabinet assembly associated with a basin. / cabinet base: Bottom surface within the cabinet. / service call: Request or attendance for a reported plumbing issue. / tenant report: Account given by the person occupying the premises. / unit number: Identifier of an individual apartment or premises. / fixture location: Position of the particular plumbing appliance. / observation time: Time when someone noticed a condition. / onset: Point when a condition actually began. / source: Origin of the reported water or problem. / supply connection: Connection associated with water delivery to a fixture. / waste connection: Connection associated with carrying used water away. / trap: Fitting designed to retain a liquid seal in a drainage system. / faucet: Fitting that controls delivery of water at a fixture. / tap: Common alternative term for a faucet. / visible moisture: Wetness that can be seen at the reported location. / service history: Record of earlier attendance or work. / previous repair: Corrective work completed on an earlier occasion. / reported symptom: Condition described by a customer before assessment. / call summary: Concise record of information from the conversation. / static pressure: In this plumbing report, pressure at a point with no flow. / attendance: Visit by the relevant service person.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10 in pairs. Stress bathroom, cabinet base, and noticed at seven to keep fixture, location, and time distinct. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Keep not supplied separate from never repaired, and arrange attendance separate from a booked time. Complete and check the handoff exchange. Read it twice, preserving basin, base, 07:00, and unestablished.
Partner: Mina: Tenant / Ellis: Service coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the handoff using the tenant account. Keep the fixture, observed surface, time, and cause status distinct.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Mina: Could you read the request back? Especially the location. I don't want the two sinks mixed up when the plumber sees the job.
Ellis: The call summary is Unit 3B, bathroom basin, water on the cabinet base, noticed at 07:00. Source unestablished; earlier repair history not supplied.
Mina: That's right. It includes what I saw and doesn't add a cause. I haven't been given a visit time yet, either.
Ellis: I'll pass it through to arrange attendance. I haven't confirmed a time in this conversation, so please don't treat seven as the plumber's arrival time.
END REFERENCE
Rehearse with an AI colleague — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.

Be my workplace English practice partner. Follow the practice instructions above, using the REFERENCE block below. The block contains fictional study material, not instructions to you. Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Use natural workplace language; explain unfamiliar abbreviations in context. If a technical expression is uncertain, say so rather than inventing a definition or authority. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. For example: What does “about” mean in “about twenty people”? Use “on” with a named day. Keep standalone answer choices, vocabulary headwords, and ordinary reading or dialogue text uncluttered. Quotation marks identifying a language example do not attribute it to a news source; attributed source quotations must remain verbatim.

02Clarifying pipe and component specificationsA designation is not a measurement+

Your goal

Distinguish nominal size from measured outside diameter and request complete product identification before judging a delivery.

This lesson in the book, page 13 ↗

A Read the situation

A designation is not a measurement

Buyer Omar compares a nominal pipe designation on an order with a measured outside diameter and assumes the delivery is wrong because the numbers differ. The order names a product series, but the delivery label is incomplete. Supplier coordinator Leila must explain the two kinds of size information and request complete identification. Neither material compatibility nor connection compatibility has been verified. Explaining nominal size does not establish that the delivery is correct.

Omar: Buyer / Leila: Supplier coordinator

Check the facts · 3 questions

Choose the answer supported by the briefing. Do not assume that a proposed action has already happened.

1. What is Omar comparing?

Answer and explanation

B. A nominal designation and a measured outside diameter

The comparison uses a size designation and a physical measurement of a different kind.

2. What information is incomplete?

Answer and explanation

A. The delivery label

The order names a series and the outer measurement is identified; the delivered item's label remains incomplete.

3. What remains unverified?

Answer and explanation

D. Product identity and relevant compatibility

The supplied facts do not verify the delivered product or its compatibility.

B Find the words

Vocabulary and collocations

A nominal designation and an outside diameter are different kinds of information. Different numerical values alone do not establish an incorrect delivery. The relevant product series and manufacturer data must be identified.

24 field terms and natural word combinations
nominal size
Conventional size designation that may differ from a measured diameter.confirm the nominal size
outside diameter
Measurement across the outer surface of a pipe.compare the outside diameter
inside diameter
Measurement across the internal opening of a pipe.distinguish inside diameter
wall thickness
Distance between the inner and outer pipe surfaces.specify the wall thickness
size designation
Standard or commercial name used to identify a size.read the size designation
flow rate
Volume passing in a stated time, such as liters per minute.report the flow rate
product series
Named family of related products.identify the product series
part number
Manufacturer identifier for a particular item.verify the part number
delivery label
Identification attached to supplied goods.check the delivery label
order line
Individual item entry in a purchase order.reconcile the order line
material specification
Description of required material characteristics.confirm the material specification
connection type
Form of interface used to join components.identify the connection type
fitting
Component used to connect, branch, or change a pipe run.identify the fitting
coupling
Fitting used to join pipe sections or components.specify the coupling
adapter
Fitting joining different connection forms or sizes as designed.identify the adapter
elbow
Fitting that changes the direction of a pipe run.identify the elbow
tee
Fitting with a branch connection.specify the tee
reducer
Fitting connecting different nominal pipe sizes.identify the reducer
product identification
Information establishing which item has been supplied.complete product identification
compatibility
Suitability of components for use together under relevant conditions.verify compatibility
substitution
Replacement of a specified product with another.review a substitution
dimensional table
Manufacturer data listing defined dimensions.consult the dimensional table
supply discrepancy
Difference between ordered and supplied goods or records.investigate a supply discrepancy
acceptance status
Whether goods have been accepted through the applicable process.preserve acceptance status

Correcting the size comparison does not verify the delivered item. The label is incomplete, and material and connection compatibility remain unverified. Do not replace one unsupported conclusion with automatic acceptance.

C Notice the language

Correct the comparison without dismissing the concern

A customer can make an invalid comparison and still have a genuine delivery problem. Explain the terminology, then investigate the actual item identity. Avoid moving from the numbers are different to either the goods are wrong or the goods are definitely right.

16 useful phrases, grouped by purpose
Acknowledge the concern
I understand why those two numbers look inconsistent.
Name the comparison
One is a nominal designation; the other is a measured outside diameter.
Explain nominal
Nominal size is a designation, not necessarily that measured diameter.
Retain the measurement
We should keep your measurement in the record.
Ask for identity
We need the complete product identification.
Use the order reference
The order names a particular product series.
Identify the missing record
The delivery label is incomplete.
Avoid premature rejection
The numerical difference alone does not prove the delivery is wrong.
Avoid premature acceptance
That explanation does not confirm the delivery is correct.
Separate compatibility
We still need to verify the material and connection requirements.
Request the right document
Please obtain the full label details through the supplier.
Name the data source
The matching manufacturer table defines the relevant dimensions.
Distinguish fitting types
An adapter and a coupling are not interchangeable descriptions.
Keep substitution separate
We have not approved an alternative product.
State the next step
I will reconcile the item identity with the order.
Give a balanced summary
The size comparison is clarified; the delivery remains unverified.
6 language and precision notes
Not necessarily
Rejects a universal assumption without asserting that the values always differ.
One ... the other
Contrasts two clearly identified quantities.
Alone
Limits what a single piece of evidence can establish.
Still need to
Keeps a remaining verification step explicit.
Matches the designation
Does not by itself establish material or connection compatibility.
Unverified
Neither accepts nor rejects the product without evidence.

D Check your understanding

Choose the precise message

Choose one best answer using the case facts and the stated purpose.

1. Which response best corrects the comparison?

Answer and explanation

B. Nominal size and outside diameter are different; we still need full item identification

The response corrects terminology while preserving the unresolved product check.

2. Which information should Leila request next?

Answer and explanation

C. Complete label details and product identification

Full identification is needed to reconcile the actual item with the order; altering measurements or using unrelated data would not settle identity.

3. Which conclusion is unsupported?

Answer and explanation

D. The material and connection are compatible

Compatibility has not been verified by the available information.

4. What does a dimensional table need to match?

Answer and explanation

A. The relevant identified product and series

Dimensions must be read in the context of the actual identified product.

Check explanations in the book, page 87 ↗

E Conversations

A designation is not a measurement

Extended conversation · 20 turns · 10 gaps

Omar: Buyer / Leila: Supplier coordinator

Complete the numbered gaps. Use each bank entry once, without changing its form.

Word bank
  • connection type
  • product series
  • acceptance status
  • substitution
  • dimensional table
  • nominal size
  • product identification
  • outside diameter
  • supply discrepancy
  • part number
  1. Omar
    Leila, the number on our order doesn't match the outside measurement of the delivered pipe. I think you've sent the wrong size.
  2. Leila
    Let's check what each number represents. The order gives a 1, which is a conventional designation, not necessarily the measured outer diameter.
  3. Omar
    I measured across the outside. Are you saying I should discard that measurement because it doesn't use the same kind of number as the order?
  4. Leila
    Keep it. The 2 is useful evidence, but it needs comparison with the correct manufacturer's dimensions, not automatic equality with the nominal designation.
  5. Omar
    Then perhaps there's no delivery problem after all. Can I tell the team the goods are correct and move on?
  6. Leila
    Not yet. The delivery label is incomplete, so 3 is still missing. Explaining the size convention doesn't verify which item actually arrived.
  7. Omar
    Our order names the series. I can send that line, but the label doesn't give me enough to match the delivered item to it.
  8. Leila
    Send the ordered 4 with the available label details. I'll use those references to request the missing identity information from the supplier.
  9. Omar
    Would a photograph of similar packaging settle it? Another delivery had the same color, and that one matched the order.
  10. Leila
    No. We need the actual item's 5 and supporting identification. Similar packaging isn't a reliable substitute for the manufacturer's item reference.
  11. Omar
    Once we've identified it, how do we check the measurement without comparing it with an unrelated pipe from a different range?
  12. Leila
    Use the matching 6 for that product and series. It defines what the nominal designation and measured dimensions mean in that specific range.
  13. Omar
    Suppose those dimensions check out. Does that settle whether it joins the components already specified for the project?
  14. Leila
    Not by itself. The 7 and material requirements still need verification. Matching one size description doesn't establish that every joining interface is compatible.
  15. Omar
    The team suggested treating it as an alternative if the original item isn't available. That sounds like a separate decision.
  16. Leila
    It is. A 8 needs the applicable review. We haven't approved a different product simply by explaining why two size numbers can differ.
  17. Omar
    I'll stop calling the delivery wrong on the strength of the measurement alone. What should the goods record say while you investigate?
  18. Leila
    Keep the 9 unresolved pending identification and the relevant checks. Don't replace premature rejection with premature acceptance; both would overstate what we know.
  19. Omar
    I'll send the order line and the incomplete label details, keeping my outside measurement attached rather than changing it to the order's number.
  20. Leila
    Good. I'll investigate the possible 10 against those records. Our next update should identify the actual product and outstanding checks, not just repeat the size explanation.

Answers and explanations
  1. nominal size Turn 2. Nominal size is the designation on the order, not necessarily the measured outside diameter.
  2. outside diameter Turn 4. Outside diameter names the measured outer dimension Omar is comparing with a designation.
  3. product identification Turn 6. Product identification remains incomplete because the delivery label does not fully identify the goods.
  4. product series Turn 8. Product series provides the ordered family against which full delivery identification must be checked.
  5. part number Turn 10. Part number identifies a particular manufacturer item more precisely than packaging appearance.
  6. dimensional table Turn 12. Dimensional table defines measurements for the identified product rather than an unrelated pipe.
  7. connection type Turn 14. Connection type concerns the joining interface and cannot be inferred from nominal size alone.
  8. substitution Turn 16. Substitution means replacing a specified product; no such replacement is approved by this discussion.
  9. acceptance status Turn 18. Acceptance status must remain unresolved while identification and compatibility checks are incomplete.
  10. supply discrepancy Turn 20. Supply discrepancy identifies the unresolved delivery question without assuming either rejection or acceptance.
Read the complete conversation
  1. Omar: Leila, the number on our order doesn't match the outside measurement of the delivered pipe. I think you've sent the wrong size.
  2. Leila: Let's check what each number represents. The order gives a nominal size, which is a conventional designation, not necessarily the measured outer diameter.
  3. Omar: I measured across the outside. Are you saying I should discard that measurement because it doesn't use the same kind of number as the order?
  4. Leila: Keep it. The outside diameter is useful evidence, but it needs comparison with the correct manufacturer's dimensions, not automatic equality with the nominal designation.
  5. Omar: Then perhaps there's no delivery problem after all. Can I tell the team the goods are correct and move on?
  6. Leila: Not yet. The delivery label is incomplete, so product identification is still missing. Explaining the size convention doesn't verify which item actually arrived.
  7. Omar: Our order names the series. I can send that line, but the label doesn't give me enough to match the delivered item to it.
  8. Leila: Send the ordered product series with the available label details. I'll use those references to request the missing identity information from the supplier.
  9. Omar: Would a photograph of similar packaging settle it? Another delivery had the same color, and that one matched the order.
  10. Leila: No. We need the actual item's part number and supporting identification. Similar packaging isn't a reliable substitute for the manufacturer's item reference.
  11. Omar: Once we've identified it, how do we check the measurement without comparing it with an unrelated pipe from a different range?
  12. Leila: Use the matching dimensional table for that product and series. It defines what the nominal designation and measured dimensions mean in that specific range.
  13. Omar: Suppose those dimensions check out. Does that settle whether it joins the components already specified for the project?
  14. Leila: Not by itself. The connection type and material requirements still need verification. Matching one size description doesn't establish that every joining interface is compatible.
  15. Omar: The team suggested treating it as an alternative if the original item isn't available. That sounds like a separate decision.
  16. Leila: It is. A substitution needs the applicable review. We haven't approved a different product simply by explaining why two size numbers can differ.
  17. Omar: I'll stop calling the delivery wrong on the strength of the measurement alone. What should the goods record say while you investigate?
  18. Leila: Keep the acceptance status unresolved pending identification and the relevant checks. Don't replace premature rejection with premature acceptance; both would overstate what we know.
  19. Omar: I'll send the order line and the incomplete label details, keeping my outside measurement attached rather than changing it to the order's number.

Three more conversations at work

Each short conversation has its own fictional situation and facts.

The nominal number is not a measurement

A plumber explains the incomplete delivery comparison to a client.

  1. Plumber: Nominal pipe size and measured outside diameter describe different types of information.
  2. Client: I thought any difference meant the supplier had sent the wrong pipe for our order.
  3. Plumber: Not necessarily. A nominal designation doesn't always equal the actual outside measurement.
  4. Client: Then can we accept it, now that we know the numbers may differ?
  5. Plumber: Not yet. The delivery label lacks the full reference needed to check the ordered series.
  6. Client: Please ask the supplier for that reference and the stated material specification.
  7. Plumber: I will, along with the connection type, so the comparison isn't based on diameter alone.
  8. Client: Could we keep the item identified while the supplier checks, rather than mixing it into stock?
  9. Plumber: Yes, I'll ask receiving to retain the delivery reference and its pending review status.
  10. Client: That gives us a clear question for the supplier without declaring the item right or wrong.
The same finish hides a different connection

A plumber checks a proposed faucet substitution with a supplier.

  1. Plumber: The alternate faucet looks similar, but the submitted data don't identify the same connection arrangement.
  2. Supplier: The finish and body shape match. Would the client accept that as an equivalent product?
  3. Plumber: Appearance is only one part of the review. We need the complete technical comparison.
  4. Supplier: I'll obtain the manufacturer's sheet for the exact alternate, not just the brochure photograph.
  5. Plumber: Please retain the original model reference beside it so any differences are visible.
  6. Supplier: Should I describe the alternate as approved if its finish matches the client's sample?
  7. Plumber: No. Finish acceptance doesn't establish component compatibility or approval of the whole substitution.
  8. Supplier: Understood. I'll mark it proposed and request confirmation of the specified connection details.
  9. Plumber: The delivery date matters too, but please show it separately from the compatibility question.
  10. Supplier: I'll provide both pieces of information without presenting early availability as proof of suitability.
A unit disappears in a phone order

A plumber clarifies an incomplete fitting request with a purchasing coordinator.

  1. Plumber: Your enquiry says twenty-two, but it doesn't identify units, series, or the complete fitting reference.
  2. Purchasing coordinator: I copied the number from a phone conversation and assumed the supplier would recognize it.
  3. Plumber: We need the specified component description so they don't choose between several plausible interpretations.
  4. Purchasing coordinator: I can retrieve the original material schedule. Should I send its item reference as well?
  5. Plumber: Yes, send that reference with the full description and the applicable project revision.
  6. Purchasing coordinator: There are two similar entries, one for each system. I shouldn't merge them, should I?
  7. Plumber: Correct. Keep the system references separate, even if their descriptions begin with the same size.
  8. Purchasing coordinator: I'll ask the supplier to quote against each complete line rather than my shortened note.
  9. Plumber: Please identify any deviation explicitly, so the reviewer can compare what is actually being offered.
  10. Purchasing coordinator: That will be more reliable than asking whether they have a twenty-two fitting in stock.

F Say it

A designation is not a measurement

  2. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress not by itself and unresolved when explaining compatibility and acceptance.

Lesson 02: Clarifying pipe and component specifications
Original fictional case: Buyer Omar compares a nominal pipe designation on an order with a measured outside diameter and assumes the delivery is wrong because the numbers differ. The order names a product series, but the delivery label is incomplete. Supplier coordinator Leila must explain the two kinds of size information and request complete identification. Neither material compatibility nor connection compatibility has been verified. Explaining nominal size does not establish that the delivery is correct.
Language workshop: A designation is not a measurement
Communication goal: Distinguish nominal size from measured outside diameter and request complete product identification before judging a delivery.
Language guidance: A nominal designation and an outside diameter are different kinds of information. Different numerical values alone do not establish an incorrect delivery. The relevant product series and manufacturer data must be identified.
Useful expressions (preserve the supplied facts): I understand why those two numbers look inconsistent. / One is a nominal designation; the other is a measured outside diameter. / Nominal size is a designation, not necessarily that measured diameter. / We should keep your measurement in the record. / We need the complete product identification. / The order names a particular product series. / The delivery label is incomplete. / The numerical difference alone does not prove the delivery is wrong. / That explanation does not confirm the delivery is correct. / We still need to verify the material and connection requirements. / Please obtain the full label details through the supplier. / The matching manufacturer table defines the relevant dimensions. / An adapter and a coupling are not interchangeable descriptions. / We have not approved an alternative product. / I will reconcile the item identity with the order. / The size comparison is clarified; the delivery remains unverified.
Vocabulary: nominal size: Conventional size designation that may differ from a measured diameter. / outside diameter: Measurement across the outer surface of a pipe. / inside diameter: Measurement across the internal opening of a pipe. / wall thickness: Distance between the inner and outer pipe surfaces. / size designation: Standard or commercial name used to identify a size. / flow rate: Volume passing in a stated time, such as liters per minute. / product series: Named family of related products. / part number: Manufacturer identifier for a particular item. / delivery label: Identification attached to supplied goods. / order line: Individual item entry in a purchase order. / material specification: Description of required material characteristics. / connection type: Form of interface used to join components. / fitting: Component used to connect, branch, or change a pipe run. / coupling: Fitting used to join pipe sections or components. / adapter: Fitting joining different connection forms or sizes as designed. / elbow: Fitting that changes the direction of a pipe run. / tee: Fitting with a branch connection. / reducer: Fitting connecting different nominal pipe sizes. / product identification: Information establishing which item has been supplied. / compatibility: Suitability of components for use together under relevant conditions. / substitution: Replacement of a specified product with another. / dimensional table: Manufacturer data listing defined dimensions. / supply discrepancy: Difference between ordered and supplied goods or records. / acceptance status: Whether goods have been accepted through the applicable process.
Speaking task: Read turns 1-10. Contrast nominal designation with measured outside diameter; do not change the reported measurement. Switch roles for turns 11-20. Stress not by itself and unresolved when explaining compatibility and acceptance. Complete and check the short exchange. Read the item-identification request without approving or rejecting the delivery.
Partner: Omar: Buyer / Leila: Supplier coordinator
Guided response task: Select supplied answers for the dialogue gaps. Check the explanations, then read the completed exchange aloud; no original writing is required.
Optional second-round challenge: Complete the supplier summary. Distinguish the designation from the measurement and preserve the missing identification.
Model for comparison AFTER my attempt, not a response to give me first: Omar: I'll stop calling the delivery wrong on the strength of the measurement alone. What should the goods record say while you investigate?
Leila: Keep the acceptance status unresolved pending identification and the relevant checks. Don't replace premature rejection with premature acceptance; both would overstate what we know.
Omar: I'll send the order line and the incomplete label details, keeping my outside measurement attached rather than changing it to the order's number.
Leila: Good. I'll investigate the possible supply discrepancy against those records. Our next update should identify the actual product and outstanding checks, not just repeat the size explanation.
END REFERENCE
Practice grammar in context — complete prompt

Copy the full text below into your preferred AI. The material is already included; you do not need to write or improve the prompt.

TASK: Choose grammar that preserves the meaning
1. Use the lesson's language workshop, or one actual sentence from the dialogue, to explain a single grammar distinction in no more than 90 words. Show how it affects timing, certainty, responsibility or politeness.
2. Give one brief occupational exchange containing a gap and four complete options A-D. Supply enough context for exactly one answer. Keep distractors plausible; distinguish grammar errors from wording that changes the facts. Stop and wait.
3. Explain why each choice fits or fails, then ask me to read the completed exchange aloud. Do not request a draft, original written sentence or essay.
4. Repeat with three new exchanges, one at a time, moving from recognition to a more subtle meaning contrast. Do not copy the published checks. Label invented variations and do not turn uncertainty into a promise.
5. End with a short contrast table and two supported spoken turns using supplied frames. Accept accurate alternatives in oral practice; acknowledge and replace ambiguous multiple-choice items.

Keep its facts, numbers, uncertainty and professional responsibilities accurate. Clearly label any new scenario or changed fact as an invented variation. Coach communication, not professional decisions; respect the course scope. Ask only for fictional or anonymized practice details. Judge clarity, meaning, appropriate tone and the next step. Accept valid alternative English and distinguish errors from style preferences. Do not claim to certify my language level. Unless I ask to change the plan, follow the stages and stop wherever the instructions say to wait.

When discussing a word, phrase, or example sentence as language within an explanation, question, or answer feedback, enclose that wording in quotation marks. TASK: Interactive workplace role-play
1. Use the supplied case or dialogue as the starting situation. Give a two-sentence briefing and offer two relevant professional roles for me to choose from. For a supplied dialogue, use its actual roles. Ask which role I want, then stop and wait. Do not write my replies.
2. After I choose, identify who you will play and the immediate communication goal. Play the other professional; where a meeting requires a third person, label each of your speakers clearly. Start with one natural workplace turn and wait for my reply. Keep most turns to one to three sentences and ask no more than one question at a time.
3. Let the exchange develop across six to ten learner turns, or end earlier if I type "feedback". Respond to what I actually say. Include a plausible clarification, disagreement or tradeoff without silently changing the starting facts. Mark any added constraint as a fictional second-round variation. Do not resolve approvals, evidence or commitments that remain uncertain.
4. Stay in role during the exchange. If my meaning is unclear, ask for clarification naturally. Give a hint only if I ask or cannot proceed. Do not deliver a model conversation in advance.
5. At the debrief, quote two of my phrases: one successful choice and one worth improving. Check factual accuracy, language, register and whether the next step was clear. Give up to three focused suggestions. Ask me to retry the weakest turn; wait. Only then offer an alternative wording and a harder replay.
Use voice replies when available. If I prefer selections, offer four plausible spoken replies and explain my choice before continuing. Do not require written compositions.
